"Two great libraries, with priceless ancient collections"—the Awqaf Library (Library of the Ministry of Religious Endowments) and the [National Library of Iraq and National Centre for Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_National_Library_and_Archive) (the House of Wisdom)—"have been burned," *The Boston Globe* reported in 2003, adding that the libraries at the University of Mosul and University of Basra had been looted. András Riedlmayer, a specialist in Islamic architecture at Harvard University,[^cite_note-283] said the U.S. State Department had asked him for advice before the invasion, and that "everybody warned them that the greatest danger was not from Tomahawk missiles but from looting." [Keith D. Waterpaugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keith_David_Watenpaugh), a specialist in Ottoman history, said, "If we are going to help the Iraqi people build a new nation, we don't do it by letting their past be destroyed."[^cite_note-284]

More serious for the post-war state of Iraq was the looting of cached weaponry and ordnance which fueled the subsequent insurgency. As many as 250,000 tons of explosives were unaccounted for by October 2004.[^cite_note-285] Disputes within the [U.S. Defense Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Defense_Department) led to delays in the post-invasion assessment and protection of Iraqi nuclear facilities. [Tuwaitha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuwaitha), the Iraqi site most scrutinized by UN inspectors since 1991, was left unguarded and was looted.[^cite_note-286][^cite_note-287]

Ancient history professor [Zainab Bahrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zainab_Bahrani) reported that a helicopter landing pad was constructed in the center of the ancient city of [Babylon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylon), and "removed layers of archeological earth from the site. The daily flights of the helicopters rattle the ancient walls and the winds created by their rotors blast sand against the fragile bricks. When my colleague \[and] I asked military personnel in charge that the helipad be shut down, the response was that it had to remain open for security reasons, for the safety of the troops."[^cite_note-Bahrani-288] Bahrani also reported that in the summer of 2004, "the wall of the Temple of Nabu and the roof of the Temple of Ninmah, both sixth century BC, collapsed as a result of the movement of helicopters."[^cite_note-Bahrani-288] Electrical power was scarce in post-war Iraq, Bahrani reported, and some fragile artifacts, including the [Ottoman](22278.md) Archive, would not survive the loss of refrigeration.[^cite_note-Bahrani-288]

## Media coverage

Main article: [Media coverage of the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_coverage_of_the_Iraq_War)

### U.S. media coverage



A study found that in the lead up to the Iraq War, most U.S. sources were overwhelmingly in favor of the invasion.

As of 2007, the invasion was the most widely and closely reported war in military history.[^cite_note-289] Television network coverage was largely pro-war and viewers were six times more likely to see a pro-war source as one who was anti-war.[^cite_note-290] *[The New York Times](30680.md)* ran a number of articles describing Saddam Hussein's attempts to build weapons of mass destruction. The 8 September 2002 article titled "U.S. Says Hussein Intensifies Quest for A-Bomb Parts" was later discredited, leading *The New York Times* to issue a public statement admitting it was not as rigorous as it should have been.[^cite_note-291]

At the start of the war, as many as 775 reporters and photographers were traveling as [embedded journalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embedded_journalists).[^cite_note-292] These reporters signed contracts with the military that limited what they were allowed to report on.[^cite_note-293] When asked why the military decided to embed journalists with the troops, Lt. Col. Rick Long of the U.S. Marine Corps replied, "Frankly, our job is to win the war. Part of that is [information warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_warfare). So we are going to attempt to dominate the information environment."[^cite_note-294]

In 2003, a study released by [Fairness & Accuracy in Reporting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairness_&_Accuracy_in_Reporting) stated the network news disproportionately focused on pro-war sources and left out many [anti-war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-war) sources. According to the study, 64% of total sources supported the war, while total anti-war sources made up 10% of the media (only 3% of U.S. sources were anti-war). The study looked only at six American news networks after 20 March for three weeks. The study stated that "viewers were more than six times as likely to see a pro-war source as one who was anti-war; with U.S. guests alone, the ratio increases to 25 to 1."[^cite_note-295]

A September 2003 poll revealed that 70% of Americans believed Saddam was involved in 9/11.[^cite_note-296] 80% of [Fox News](11121.md) viewers were found to hold at least one such belief about the invasion, compared to 23% of [PBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PBS) viewers.[^cite_note-297] [Ted Turner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Turner), founder of [CNN](62028.md), charged that [Rupert Murdoch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert_Murdoch) was using Fox News to advocate an invasion.[^cite_note-298] Critics argued that this statistic is indicative of misleading coverage by the U.S. media, since viewers in other countries were less likely to have these beliefs.[^cite_note-299] A post-2008 election poll by [FactCheck.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FactCheck.org) found that 48% of Americans believed Saddam played a role in 9/11, the group concluded that "voters, once deceived, tend to stay that way despite all evidence."[^cite_note-300]

### Independent media coverage

[Independent media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_media) also played a prominent role in covering the invasion. The [Indymedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indymedia) network, among many other independent networks including many journalists from the invading countries, provided reports on the Iraq war. In the U.S., the radio program *[Democracy Now](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_Now)* was critical of the invasion, and crimes committed by the U.S. in Iraq.

The [Israeli Military Censor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Military_Censor) have released gag orders to Fresh and Rotter news platforms preventing them releasing any information about events and action related to the invasion.[^cite_note-301]

On the other side, among media not opposing to the invasion, *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* reported that "the normal diplomatic tools—sanctions, persuasion, pressure, UN resolutions—have all been tried, during 12 deadly but failed years" then giving a mild conditional support to the war, stating that if Saddam "refuses to disarm, it would be right to go to war".[^cite_note-302]

Australian [war artist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_artist) [George Gittoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Gittoes) collected independent interviews with soldiers while producing his documentary *[Soundtrack To War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soundtrack_To_War)*. The Iraq War provided the first time in history that military on the front lines were able to provide direct, uncensored reportage themselves, thanks to blogging software and the reach of the internet. Dozens of such reporting sites, known as [soldier blogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warblog) or milblogs, were started during the war. These blogs were more often than not largely pro-war and stated various reasons why the soldiers and Marines felt they were doing the right thing.[^cite_note-303]

### International media coverage

International coverage of the war differed from coverage in the U.S. in a number of ways. Arab-language news channel [Al Jazeera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Jazeera_Arabic) and the German satellite channel [Deutsche Welle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deutsche_Welle) featured almost twice as much information on the political background of the war.[^cite_note-304] Al Jazeera also showed scenes of civilian casualties and insurgent attacks rarely seen in the U.S. media.

## Criticism

Main article: [Criticism of the Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_the_Iraq_War)



Anti war protest in London, 2002

Opponents of the invasion criticized it by referring the human cost of war; by arguing that the war was [illegal](#Legality_of_invasion), that little evidence could justify it, that Iraq could still be negotiated with, or that the U.S. had more pressing security priorities (i.e., [Afghanistan](737.md) and [North Korea](21255.md)); and by predicting that it would destabilize the Middle East.[^cite_note-305]

### Rationale based on faulty evidence

The central U.S. justification for launching the war was that Saddam's alleged development of nuclear and biological weapons and purported ties to al-Qaeda made his regime a "grave and growing"[^cite_note-306] threat to the U.S. and international community.[^cite_note-307] During the lead-up to the war and the aftermath of the invasion, critics cast doubt on the evidence supporting this rationale. Concerning Iraq's weapons programs, prominent critics included [Scott Ritter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Ritter), a former UN weapons inspector who argued in 2002 that inspections had eliminated the nuclear and chemical weapons programs, and that evidence of their reconstitution would "have been eminently detectable by intelligence services ..." Although it is popularly believed that Saddam had forced the IAEA weapons inspectors to leave Iraq, they were withdrawn at the request of the U.S. before Operation Desert Fox. After the build-up of U.S. troops in neighboring states, Saddam welcomed them back and promised complete cooperation with their demands. Experienced IAEA inspection teams were already back in Iraq and had made some interim reports on its search for various forms of WMD.[^cite_note-308][^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310][^cite_note-311][^cite_note-312] American diplomat [Joseph C. Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_C._Wilson) investigated the contention that Iraq had sought uranium for nuclear weapons in [Niger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger_uranium_forgeries) and reported that the contention had no substance.[^cite_note-313][^cite_note-314]

Similarly, alleged links between Iraq and al-Qaeda were called into question during the lead-up to the war, and were discredited by a 2004 report from U.S. Senator [Carl Levin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Levin), which was later corroborated by a 2006 report from the Defense Department's inspector general.[^cite_note-315] These reports further alleged that Bush administration officials, particularly former undersecretary of defense [Douglas J. Feith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_J._Feith), manipulated evidence to support links between al-Qaeda and Iraq.[^cite_note-316]

During his 2003–2004 interrogation, Saddam asserted that the majority of Iraq's WMD stockpiles had been destroyed in the 1990s by UN inspectors, and the remainder were destroyed unilaterally by Iraq; the illusion of maintaining a WMD program and WMDs was maintained as a deterrent against possible Iranian invasion.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-317] An FBI agent who interrogated Saddam at this time stated in 2008 that while Iraq may not have possessed WMDs after the 1990s, Saddam likely intended to restart the WMD program if given the opportunity to do so.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-317]

### Lack of a United Nations mandate

One of the main questions in the lead-up to the war was whether the UNSC would authorize military intervention in Iraq. It became increasingly clear that this authorization would require significant further weapons inspections. Many criticized their effort as unwise, immoral, and illegal. [Robin Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robin_Cook), then-leader of the [UK House of Commons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_House_of_Commons) and a former foreign secretary, resigned from Blair's cabinet in protest over the UK's decision to invade without UN authorization. Cook said at the time that: "In principle, I believe it is wrong to embark on military action without broad international support. In practice, I believe it is against Britain's interests to create a precedent for unilateral military action."[^cite_note-318] In addition, senior government legal advisor [Elizabeth Wilmshurst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_Wilmshurst) resigned, stating her legal opinion that an invasion would be illegal.

[UN Secretary-General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Secretary-General) [Kofi Annan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kofi_Annan) said in September 2004, "\[F]rom our point of view, and from the Charter point of view, \[the war] was illegal."[^cite_note-319] This drew immediate criticism from the U.S., and was immediately played down.[^cite_note-320] His annual report to the [General Assembly](31957.md) for 2003 included no more than the statement: "Following the end of major hostilities which resulted in the occupation of Iraq..."[^cite_note-UN_A581-321] A similar report from the UNSC was similarly terse in its reference to the event: "Following the cessation of hostilities in Iraq in April 2003 ... \[we have] passed nearly 60 resolutions on Iraq and Kuwait since Iraq's invasion of Kuwait in 1990. The most relevant to this issue is Resolution 678".[^cite_note-UN_A582-322]

### Military intervention vs. diplomatic solution

Wikisource logo

English [Wikisource](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikisource) has original text related to this article:&#x20;

**[French address on Iraq at the UN Security Council](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/en\:French%20address%20on%20Iraq%20at%20the%20UN%20Security%20Council)**

Criticisms about the evidence used to justify the war notwithstanding, many opponents of military intervention objected, saying that a diplomatic solution would be preferable, and that war should be reserved as a truly last resort. This position was exemplified by French Foreign Minister [Dominique de Villepin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominique_de_Villepin), who responded to Powell's UNSC presentation by saying that: "Given the choice between military intervention and an inspections regime that is inadequate because of a failure to cooperate on Iraq's part, we must choose the decisive reinforcement of the means of inspections."[^cite_note-323] In response to Rumsfeld's reference to European countries that did not support the invasion of Iraq as 'Old Europe',[^cite_note-324] Dominique de Villepin replied: "This message comes to you today from an old country, France, from a continent like mine, Europe, that has known wars, occupation and barbarity. (...) Faithful to its values, it wishes resolutely to act with all the members of the international community. It believes in our ability to build together a better world."[^cite_note-325] The direct opposition between diplomatic solution and military intervention involving France and the U.S. which was personified by Chirac versus Bush, and later Powell versus de Villepin, became a milestone in the [U.S.–France relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-American_relations). [Anti-French propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francophobia) exploiting Francophobic clichés immediately ensued in the [U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-French_sentiment_in_the_United_States) and UK. A call for a boycott on French wine was launched in the U.S. The *[New York Post](102227.md)* claimed that France had forgotten the American "sacrifice" in 1944 France. On 20 February 2003, British newspaper *[The Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sun_\(United_Kingdom\))* published a special issue entitled "Chirac is a worm", which said that "Chirac has become the shame of Europe".[^cite_note-ina.fr-20030220-326] *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* wrote that both papers expressed the opinion of their owner, [Rupert Murdoch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert_Murdoch).[^cite_note-ina.fr-20030220-326][^cite_note-327]

### Distraction from the war on terrorism and other priorities

Both supporters and opponents of the Iraq War widely viewed it within the context of a post–9/11 world, where the U.S. has sought to make terrorism the defining international security paradigm. Bush often described the war as a "central front in the [war on terror](13425800.md)".[^cite_note-328] Some critics of the war, particularly within the U.S. military community, argued pointedly against the conflation of Iraq and the war on terror, and criticized Bush for losing focus on the more important objective of fighting al-Qaeda. As Marine Lieutenant General [Greg Newbold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greg_Newbold), the Pentagon's former top operations officer, wrote in a 2006, "I now regret that I did not more openly challenge those who were determined to invade a country whose actions were peripheral to the real threat—al-Qaeda."[^cite_note-329]

Critics within this vein have further argued that containment would have been an effective strategy for the Saddam government, and that the top U.S. priorities in the Middle East should be encouraging a solution to the [Israeli–Palestinian conflict](46216.md), working for the moderation of [Iran](14653.md), and solidifying gains made in [Afghanistan](19666611.md) and Central Asia. In an October 2002 speech, Retired Marine Gen. [Anthony Zinni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Zinni), former head of [Central Command](338482.md) for U.S. forces in the Middle East and State Department's envoy to the Israeli–Palestinian conflict, called Iraq "maybe six or seven," in terms of U.S. Middle East priorities, adding that "the affordability line may be drawn around five."[^cite_note-330] However, while commander of CENTCOM, Zinni held a very different opinion concerning the threat posed by Iraq. In testimony before the Senate Armed Services Committee in February 2000, Zinni said: "Iraq remains the most significant near-term threat to U.S. interests in the Persian Gulf region. This is primarily due to its large conventional military force, pursuit of WMD, oppressive treatment of Iraqi citizens, refusal to comply with \[UNSC resolutions], persistent threats to enforcement of the No Fly Zones (NFZ), and continued efforts to violate UN Security Council sanctions through oil smuggling."[^cite_note-331] Zinni specifically referred to "the Persian Gulf region" in his Senate testimony, which is a significantly smaller region of the world than the "Middle East", which he referred to in 2007.

### Potential to destabilize the region

Besides arguing that Iraq was not the top strategic priority in the war on terrorism or in the Middle East, critics of the war also suggested that it could potentially destabilize the surrounding region. Prominent among such critics was [Brent Scowcroft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brent_Scowcroft), who served as [National Security Advisor](44028.md) to [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush). In a 15 August 2002 *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* editorial entitled "Don't attack [Saddam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam)", Scowcroft wrote that, "Possibly the most dire consequences would be the effect in the region ... there would be an explosion of outrage against us ... the results could well destabilize Arab regimes", and, "could even swell the ranks of the terrorists."[^cite_note-332] In 2015, Blair apologized for his "mistakes" over Iraq War and admitted there were "elements of truth" to the view that the invasion helped promote the rise of [ISIS](9087364.md).[^cite_note-333] In the opinion of Hayder al-Khoei, Iraq was already "destined for chaos" before 2003.[^cite_note-334]

## Public opinion

In a March 2003 [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_\(company\)) poll, the day after the invasion, 76% of Americans supported the military action against Iraq.[^cite_note-335] In a March [YouGov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouGov) poll, 54% of Britons had approved of military action against Iraq.[^cite_note-336]

By 2007, opposition to the Iraq War had increased to 62% among Americans in a [USA Today](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USA_Today)\Gallup poll.[^cite_note-337] In 2013, a Gallup poll found that 53 percent of Americans surveyed believed the Iraq War was a mistake.[^cite_note-338]

In 2023, an [Axios](52436164.md)/[Ipsos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipsos) poll found that 61% of Americans believed the U.S made a wrong decision by invading Iraq.[^cite_note-339]

## Related phrases



Poland was part of the "coalition of the willing"

This campaign featured a variety of new terminology, much of it initially coined by the U.S. government or military. The military official name for the invasion was Operation Iraqi Freedom. Also notable was the usage "[death squads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_squad)" to refer to Fedayeen paramilitary forces. Iraqi officials were given derogatory nicknames – e.g., "Chemical Ali" ([Ali Hassan al-Majid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Hassan_al-Majid)), "Baghdad Bob" or "Comical Ali" ([Muhammed Saeed al-Sahaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammed_Saeed_al-Sahaf)), and "Mrs. Anthrax" or "Chemical Sally" ([Huda Salih Mahdi Ammash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huda_Salih_Mahdi_Ammash)).

Terminology introduced or popularized during the war include:

- "[Axis of evil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_of_evil)", originally used by Bush in his [2002 State of the Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_State_of_the_Union) speech on 29 January 2002 to refer to the countries of Iran, Iraq, and North Korea.

  [^cite_note-340]

- "[Coalition of the willing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_of_the_willing)", a term that originated in the Clinton era, and used by the Bush administration for the countries contributing troops in the invasion, of which the U.S. and UK were the primary members.

- "[Decapitating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decapitation_strike) the regime", a euphemism for killing Saddam Hussein.

- "[Embedding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embedded_journalist)", the American practice of assigning civilian journalists to U.S. military units.

- "[Freedom fries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_fries)", a euphemism for [French fries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_fries) invented to protest the non-participation of France.

- "[Mother of all bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_of_all_bombs)", a bomb developed and produced to support Operation Iraqi Freedom. Its name echoed Saddam's phrase "[Mother of all battles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_of_all_battles)" to describe the Gulf War.

  [^cite_note-341]

- "[Old Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Europe_\(politics\))", Rumsfeld's term for European governments not supporting the war: "You're thinking of Europe as Germany and France. I don't. I think that's old Europe."

- "[Regime change](154074.md)", a euphemism for overthrowing a government.

- "[Shock and awe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shock_and_awe)", the strategy of reducing an enemy's will to fight through displays of overwhelming force.

Many slogans and terms coined came to be used by Bush's political opponents, or those opposed to the war. For example, in April 2003 John Kerry said at a campaign rally: "What we need now is not just a regime change in Saddam Hussein and Iraq, but we need a regime change in the United States."[^cite_note-342]

Bush's press secretary [Ari Fleischer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ari_Fleischer) talked about "Operation Iraqi Liberation" in a 2003 press briefing,[^cite_note-Fleischer-OIL-343] and "Operation Iraqi Liberation (OIL)" was also used by [David Rovics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Rovics), a popular folk protest singer.

The Awqaf Library (Library of the Ministry of Religious En- dowments), which contained 8,500 Islamic manuscript codices (an ancient type of book) in Ara-bic, as well as hundreds of manu-scripts in Persian and Ottoman Turkish. It also contained ancient illuminated Korans. Its oldest work was a scriptural commen-tary by the ninth-century scholar Ibn Qutayba, copied in 1079. The National Library of Iraq and National Centre for Archives, known as the House of Wisdom, comparable to the US Library of Congress. It held 417,000 books, 2,618 periodicals from the late Ottoman era and modern times, and a collection of 4,412 rare books and manuscripts.
